## Página 182

CAPÍTULO 7. ARANDULAND HOJE E AMANHÃ! 181
Aranduland investe também em comunidades vivas e conectadas. As comunidades da
cidade são modelos de inclusão, diversidade e economia solidária. Elas produzem, apren-
dem e compartilham. Cada bairro é uma célula criativa, com hortas urbanas, cooperativas
tecnológicas e espaços de convivência, impulsionando os ODS 1 (Erradicação da Pobreza),
ODS 10 (Redução das Desigualdades) e ODS 11 (Cidades Sustentáveis).
Aranduland é o encontro entre o campo inteligente e a comunidade empoderada. É o
agro que respeita e a comunidade que cresce.
Figura 7.6: AGRO  ODS 2.
Fonte: O autor, 2025.
3 Uma cidade onde a Diversidade, Inclusão e Igualdade são reais
Aranduland nasceu com um propósito claro: ser um espaço de aprendizado, convivência
e futuro  para todos, sem exceção.
Aqui, a diversidade é celebrada como força vital da cidade. Pessoas de diferentes culturas,
origens, idades, gêneros, raças, habilidades e crenças convivem, aprendem e cocriam jun-
tas. Em cada bairro, praça ou local de Aranduland, você vai encontrar representatividade,
respeito e pluralidade. A inovação só é completa quando inclui todas as vozes.
A inclusão é uma prática diária. Aranduland está sendo construída com acessibilidade,
tanto física quanto digital. Os cursos, jogos e experiências da cidade são pensados para
abraçar todos os pers, com linguagem clara e suporte inteligente. Pessoas com deci-
ência, populações indígenas, imigrantes virtuais, LGBTQIA+, idosos e jovens têm seu
espaço e seu protagonismo.
E acima de tudo, a igualdade é um compromisso real. Em Aranduland, oportunidades

não são privilégio  são direito. Todos têm acesso à educação, cultura, saúde, ao empre-
endedorismo e à expressão. Acreditamos em justiça social e em equidade ativa: corrigir
desigualdades históricas é parte do caminho.
Com base nos ODS 5 (Igualdade de Gênero), ODS 10 (Redução das Desigualdades) e
ODS 16 (Paz, Justiça e Instituições Ecazes) e, principalmente ODS 1  Erradicação da

Aranduland é um modelo de cidade: Diversa. Inclusiva. Igualitária.
Uma cidade onde ninguém ca para trás  e todos caminham juntos.

4 Energia limpa, cidade inteligente, gerando uma vida sustentável
Aranduland é uma cidade que pretende antecipar o que o mundo precisa alcançar amanhã.
Foi projetada para ser inteligente, humana e sustentável, unindo inovação tecnológica,
responsabilidade ambiental e qualidade de vida.
No coração dessa transformação está o uso intensivo de energias alternativas. A cidade
pretende funcionar com uma matriz energética limpa e diversicada: energia solar nos
telhados, eólica nos parques, biogás proveniente de resíduos orgânicos, hidrogênio verde
para mobilidade pesada e até miniusinas nucleares seguras e ecientes para garantir au-
tonomia energética em escala urbana. Tudo isso com rastreabilidade, controle em tempo
real e integração com sistemas de consumo consciente. Ver Figura 7.8.

Em Aranduland a inteligência urbana está em cada decisão. Sensores (IoT), dados e
inteligência articial garantem a eciência de serviços públicos, mobilidade, iluminação,
segurança e gestão de recursos naturais. Cada rua e cada quarteirão são planejados para
reduzir o impacto ambiental e aumentar o bem-estar coletivo.
A moradia sustentável é outro pilar essencial. Os prédios progressivamente vão seguir
princípios de construção verde, com materiais reutilizáveis, captação de água da chuva,
painéis solares, ventilação natural e certicações ambientais. Vamos ter um sistema de
transportes exemplo de mobilidade do século XXI: modais interligados, veículos elétricos,
ciclovias inteligentes, corredores verdes para pedestres e integração com transporte público
automatizado. Carros autônomos, ônibus elétricos e trens suspensos  tudo conectado
por uma infraestrutura digital que reduz o tempo, o custo e as emissões.
Aranduland se alinha integralmente aos ODS 7 (Energia Limpa e Acessível), ODS 9
(Indústria, Inovação e Infraestrutura), ODS 11 (Cidades e Comunidades Sustentáveis) e
ODS 13 (Ação Contra a Mudança Global do Clima).
É mais que uma cidade. É um modelo replicável de futuro sustentável.
Energia limpa. Moradia digna. Transporte eciente.

interativas, jardins terapêuticos, trilhas ecológicas, espaços de meditação e centros de
conexão humana fazem de Aranduland uma cidade onde se vive com mais leveza e sentido.
O bem-estar aqui é emocional, espiritual, social e ambiental.

Em Aranduland, acreditamos que não pode haver futuro sustentável sem paz duradoura,
justiça acessível e instituições transparentes e ecazes. Mais do que uma cidade virtual,
Aranduland é um território de esperança e reconstrução de valores. Aqui, a paz é cultivada
desde cedo, por meio da educação para a empatia, da resolução pacíca de conitos, da
escuta ativa e do fortalecimento da cultura do cuidado.
Nas escolas, nos fóruns públicos e nas experiências imersivas, os habitantes podem apren-
der que a verdadeira segurança nasce da inclusão e da conança mútua.
A representação da justiça em Aranduland deve ser acessível e orientada pelos direitos
humanos. Tribunais-modelo, mediação comunitária e conselhos participativos garantem
que cada voz seja ouvida. Não há privilégios, apenas equidade, onde a transparência é
uma regra.

abertos, participação cidadã e auditoria social. O ODS 16 (Paz, Justiça e Instituições
Ecazes) presente!

## Página 189

188
# Capítulo 8
# (Data) Science for Social Justice:
# Territorial Education and Tools for Smart
# and Equitable Cities
1
Autor: Karla Patricia Oliveira - Esquerre, Ph. D.
Instituição: Full Professor Federal University of Bahia, Polytechnic School of
Engineering, Graduate Program of Industrial Engineering (PEI),
Graduate Program in Energy and Environment (PPGEAm);
Gamma Lab. leader (https://gamma.ufba.br/).
e-mail: karlaesquerre@ufba.br
This chapter explores the transformative role of data science education in advancing civic enga-
gement and social justice. Building on the Data Science in Public Education (DSPE) program
and its agship initiatives across diverse learning environments, it challenges technocentric visi-
ons of smart cities by emphasizing territorial education, critical data literacy, and participatory
governance. Spanning middle school to graduate studies, the initiative embeds data science
within multiple educational levels and local contexts, aligning with Brazil's smart city agen-
das. Through case studies, teacher training models, and community-driven tools such as the
(re)Conhecendo Salvador platform, the chapter illustrates how inclusive approaches to data
education can foster Society 5.0where technology is mobilized to serve human needs rather
than exacerbate inequalities. Insights from this six-year program provide a practical blueprint
for educators, policymakers, and researchers committed to shaping just and intelligent cities
grounded in the voices and agency of youth.

---

## Página 190

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 189
8.1 BEYOND TECHNOCENTRIC VISION
Contemporary discourses on Smart Cities are often dominated by a form of technological
determinism in which sensors, automated systems, AI, and data centers are positioned as the
main sources of urban intelligence (Alizadeh; Shari, 2023; Sharma; Hargreaves; Pallett, 2023).
However, when not accompanied by epistemic democratization and citizen participation, these
infrastructures tend to reproduce pre-existing social inequalities. Many implementations priori-
tize infrastructure over inclusion, reducing citizen engagement to symbolic consultation rather
than meaningful co-creation (Eubanks, 2018). As a result, smart divides emerge, deepening
disparities in digital access and data literacy (Gurstein, 2011), see Figure 8.1. This is especi-
ally critical in Global South contexts, where standardized smart city models often neglect local
knowledge and community-based environmental practices (Cruz; Silva, 2023). For example, the
ecological footprint of smart technologiesfrom energy-intensive data centers to the rapid ob-
solescence of hardwarecan undermine their supposed sustainable benets (Oliveira-Esquerre
et al., 2021; Yigitcanlar et al., 2019; Sharma; Hargreaves; Pallett, 2023; Wang et al., 2024).
Recent research reinforces these concerns: Kolotouchkina, Ripoll González e Belabas (2024) de-
monstrate how smart city implementations can exacerbate digital inequalities when inclusion is
not prioritized, and Donio e Toch (2025) highlight neighborhood-based disparities in adopting
smart city services, driven by dierences in digital prociency and privacy perceptions.
Figura 8.1: Smart Cities and Equity: balancing technological innovation with social justice.
The illustration highlights the coexistence of advanced digital infrastructures and marginalized
urban contexts, emphasizing the central role of equity in shaping inclusive futures.
Fonte: co-created by the author and Maíra Oliveira Esquerre (2025).
This chapter proposes an alternative starting point: instead of simply connecting devices,
Smart Cities must connect peopleto knowledge, to data, and to their own territories. It is not
enough for a city to be smart in computational terms; it must be smart in its ability to include,

## Página 191

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 190
to listen, and to respond to the needs of its inhabitants (Santos; Bijos; Fernandes et al., 2025;
Santos; Bijos; Pessoa et al., 2025). From this perspective, this discussion should be integrated
into the concept of Society 5.0, which seeks to create an intelligent society centered on the
human being, considering technology as a tool for addressing social challenges and improving
quality of life (Adel; Hs Alani, 2024). Based on this idea, education should be understood
not as a peripheral function but as a central infrastructure of a Smart City. This view is
especially urgent in Latin America, Africa and Global South countries, where the acceleration
of technological agendas often contrasts with persistent decits in educational equity and social
justice.
Data literacy has shown great potential for developing critical and creative thinking skills.
Some innovative initiatives have already begun to explore the integration of global problems
data in education. Table 8.1 highlights dierent contemporary data education initiatives that
are adapting their approaches regionally to integrate real-world issues. Promoting discussions
on these topics enables young people to understand how data is collected, analyzed, and used
in a wide variety of contexts. By equipping youth with these skills, active participation in the
creation and management of smart cities is encouraged, ensuring that these urban environments
are inclusive, ecient, and sustainable (Parnell et al., 2023). This empowerment not only
increases individual autonomy but also strengthens the community, making it capable of making
informed decisions and thus contributing to the overall success of smart city initiatives.

## Página 192

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 191
In recent years, the growing availability of free and open-access resources has inuenced
the way data science is taught and learned, not only through technology but also through
the communities, cultures, and local realities that shape their use. Beyond reducing nancial
and technical barriers, these resources can strengthen learners' ability to connect data to is-
sues that matter in their own contextsencouraging inquiry into local challenges, community
histories, and lived experiences. When used alongside collaborative and locally relevant practi-
ceswhether through cloud-based platforms, publicly available datasets, open-source tools, or
peer learning circlesthese resources can help create learning environments where theoretical
knowledge is meaningfully connected to practical, community-driven applications. Moreover,
creative approaches that adapt these resources to local conditionssuch as oine materials,
culturally relevant examples, and partnerships with community organizationscan support
meaningful learning even in low-connectivity or under-resourced settings, making data literacy
more than a technical skill, but a tool for critical engagement with the world.
When integrating these resources and approaches into K12 education, several key con-
siderations emerge that go beyond technical access. The rst challenge is age-appropriate
scaolding: for younger students, this means guided exploration and examples grounded in
familiar cultural and community contexts; for older students, this might involve more auto-
nomous, project-based work that addresses issues relevant to their own lives. Dierences in
digital literacy can also limit engagement, as some learners may be unfamiliar not only with
the concepts but also with the ways digital tools are embedded in learninghighlighting the
need to integrate oine, discussion-based, and hands-on activities that build condence and
critical thinking. Furthermore, integrating these approaches into rigid school curricula often
requires negotiation with national learning standards, which may not yet explicitly recognize
data science but can still be addressed through cross-disciplinary links to mathematics, social
studies, environmental science, and civic education. Teachers often need to balance screen time
with unplugged, collaborative, and community-based activities to sustain focusparticularly
for younger learnerswhile also ensuring that datasets and analytical tasks remain accessible
and rooted in issues that feel real and important to students.
The challenges become even greater in communities where opportunities to engage with
digital culture are scarce for both students and teachers. In such contexts, learners may have
minimal exposure to data-informed reasoning, limiting their ability to connect information to
their lived experiences or to question how and why certain narratives are constructed with
data. Teachers, in turn, may lack the training, condence, and institutional support to bring
data-related inquiry into their classrooms, sometimes relying on outdated materials or avoiding
the subject altogethernot due to disinterest, but because they face competing priorities and
limited professional learning opportunities. These gaps perpetuate inequities, as students in

## Página 193

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 192
under-resourced schools miss chances to develop analytical, problem-solving, and civic reasoning
skills that are increasingly important for navigating both higher education and democratic
participation. Without targeted professional development, institutional support, and sustained
opportunities to connect learning with community concerns, both teachers and students risk
exclusion from the conversations and decisions shaping a data-rich worlddeepening social and
knowledge divides that open access alone cannot resolve.
The city of Salvadorone of Brazil's most socially complex capitalsprovides both the
empirical foundation and the conceptual lens for this investigation. Celebrated for its Afro-
Brazilian cultural heritage, Salvador also embodies stark inequities in education that mirror
broader disparities in infrastructure, teacher training, and curriculum implementation. Limited
internet access, inadequate teacher preparation, and an overreliance on basic digital tools have
combined to exclude many marginalized studentsparticularly Black, rural, and low-income
populationsfrom developing the skills needed to navigate an increasingly algorithmic world
(Gurstein, 2011) At the same time, broader policy agendas such as the Plano Diretor de Cidades
Inteligentes do Estado da Bahia (SEMIT, 2022) chart long-term strategies to transform Bahia
into a smart region over the next 30 years. Developed by the Secretaria de Inovação e Tecnologia
(SEMIT), this plansimilar to initiatives in other Brazilian states and municipalitiesframes
connectivity and innovation as central drivers of urban transformation, highlighting the tension
between ambitious smart city visions and persistent educational inequities on the ground.
This chapter explores the Data Science in Public Education (DSPE) program and
its innovative methodology for transforming data science education into a vehicle for social
justice. By integrating data literacy across middle and high schoolsas well as vocational,
undergraduate, and graduate curricula, with selected agship initiatives presented in Section
5the program empowers youth with critical analytical skills while addressing systemic ine-
quities. This chapter also highlights how this emerging framework connects classroom learning
to real-world challengesfrom urban inequality to environmental justiceequipping students
to harness data as a tool for civic engagement and advocacy. Through case studies and parti-
cipatory approaches, it is demonstrated how centering youth voices in data science education
can challenge traditional power structures and foster more equitable urban futures.
8.2 WHAT IS SOCIAL JUSTICE IN THE AGE OF SMART
CITIES?
The concept of social justice long predates the digital era, emerging in the 19th century
through thinkers like Taparelli (1860); and later rened by philosophers such as Rawls (1971)
and Behr (2019). Historically, it has anchored struggles for redistributive fairness, universal
A Linguagem R na Sociedade 5.0 & Smart Cities Portal de Livros Abertos da USP,
Pirassununga: Faculdade de Zootecnia e Engenharia de Alimentos, 2026. 211 p. ISBN
978-65-87023-55-7 (e-book). Disponível em: https://doi.org/10.11606/9786587023557.

---

## Página 194

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 193
access to education, protection of labor rights, and the dismantling of systemic inequities. In
Latin America, Africa, and other Global South regions, it has also been closely intertwined with
land reform, racial justice, feminist activism, and decolonial movementspolitical currents that
have sought to construct more inclusive and equitable societies.
With the rise of smart cities as dominant models for urban development, the term social
justice has been increasingly reframed through the lens of digitalization. While these newer
interpretations often focus on broadband access, user-friendly platforms, and equitable deli-
very of smart services, such a narrow scope risks stripping the concept of its deeper political,
economic, and cultural dimensions (Cardullo; Kitchin, 2022). However, an intersectional pers-
pective is indispensable for understanding how racial, gender, economic, and spatial systems of
oppression interact to reproduce inequalities (Sharma; Hargreaves; Pallett, 2023) something
technocentric narratives can obscure when detached from systemic analysis.
Within this shift, data-oriented education becomes a critical enabler of genuine social
justice. In contexts where digital tools are rapidly deployed without matching investments
in skills and literacy, access alone is insucient. Evidence from South Africawhere deep
structural inequalities persist due to its socio-political historymirrors challenges seen across
the Global South, including Brazil, India, and parts of Southeast Asia. The deployment of
technologies like IoT, big data analytics, and AI can signicantly improve eciency in energy,
transportation, healthcare, and public safety. However, without inclusive governance and deli-
berate redistributive policies, these advancements risk reinforcing existing divides. Smart city
strategies must address the digital divide in all its formsaccess to devices, aordability of
connectivity, digital literacy, and the ability to use data tools meaningfullywhile also meeting
the needs of vulnerable groups such as women, the elderly, ethnic minorities, and people with
disabilities (Ntanda; Carolissen, 2025). Without these capacities, communities are excluded
from decision-making and denied the means to hold institutions accountable.
Real social justice in the age of smart cities therefore demands more than equitable tech-
nology distribution; it requires structural reforms that redistribute power, recognize historically
marginalized voices, and redesign urban systems to redress spatial, racial, and socio-economic
inequities. This includes embedding participatory governance mechanisms that go beyond con-
sultation, ensuring that communities directly inuence decisions about infrastructure, data
governance, and public service priorities. Addressing territorial equity is essential to close
the gap between auent, digitally integrated districts and peripheral or informal settlements
lacking basic infrastructure. In this regard, data-oriented education functions as both a right
and a tool of empowerment: it equips citizens to interpret urban data, identify inequities, and
advocate for transformative policy changes.
Seen through this lens, technocentric urban visions risk overlooking systemic issues such
A Linguagem R na Sociedade 5.0 & Smart Cities Portal de Livros Abertos da USP,
Pirassununga: Faculdade de Zootecnia e Engenharia de Alimentos, 2026. 211 p. ISBN
978-65-87023-55-7 (e-book). Disponível em: https://doi.org/10.11606/9786587023557.

## Página 195

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 194
as mobility inequities, where low-income residents spend disproportionate amounts of time and
income commuting, or environmental injustices, where marginalized communities face greater
exposure to pollution and climate hazards. The rapid roll-out of surveillance technologies in
the name of safety can also disproportionately target or stigmatize poorer and racialized po-
pulations, underscoring the need for privacy protections and rights-based frameworks (Sharma;
Hargreaves; Pallett, 2023). Well-designed, inclusive data-oriented education can counteract
these trends by enabling citizens to understand the implications of such technologies and ac-
tively participate in shaping their use. The contrast between a technocentric smart city and
one guided by inclusive, data-oriented principles is stark: while the former can entrench exis-
ting inequalities, the latter can leverage technology as a catalyst for equity and democratic
engagement, as summarized in Table 8.2. This contrast is also illustrated in Figure 8.2, which
visually depicts the dierence between a surveillance-driven, technocentric smart city and a
participatory, inclusive smart city grounded in data-oriented education.
Tabela 8.2: Contrast between the technocentric smart city versus one guided by inclusive, data-
oriented principles.

Figura 8.2: Contrasting models of smart cities: on the left, a technocentric model emphasizing

Ultimately, as both historical experience and contemporary research indicate, the trans-
formative promise of smart cities will only be realized if technological innovation is matched
by policies that actively dismantle inequality. In this sense, the concept of a societal smart
city must integrate social rights, democratic values, and social justice into its very foundation
(Alizadeh; Shari, 2023) and critical scholars, for instance, should focus on broader questions
of urban justice in a digital age (Rosol; Blue, 2022). This requires sustained investment in
digital literacy programs and publicprivate partnerships to expand aordable infrastructure,
and robust mechanisms for citizen oversight. In South Africa, as in Brazil and other Glo-
bal South contexts, strategies that promote social equality and economic growth by creating
jobs, empowering vulnerable groups, and providing targeted education programs are essential
(Ntanda; Carolissen, 2025). For each regional context, active citizen participation can enhance
government accountability, and these strategies, when grounded in data literacy and equitable
governance, oer a pathway to address urbanization challenges and promote both social justice
and economic development. Without such intentionality, smart cities may merely become more
ecient at perpetuating exclusion, hiding old inequalities behind new interfaces.
8.3 THE SMART CITY FRAMEWORK: KEY ELEMENTS AND
INDICATORS
The implementation of smart cities constitutes a complex system that involves multiple
aspects and requires more than just the adoption of technology. It demands a clear set of
A Linguagem R na Sociedade 5.0 & Smart Cities Portal de Livros Abertos da USP,
Pirassununga: Faculdade de Zootecnia e Engenharia de Alimentos, 2026. 211 p. ISBN

## Página 197

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 196
principles, domains, and indicators to guide urban planning in an inclusive and sustainable
manner. To ensure that the social justice values discussed in the previous sections are translated
into concrete actions, it is essential to have frameworks that allow for the evaluation and
guidance of urban development across multiple dimensions  economic, social, environmental,
and governance.
In recent years, dierent smart city frameworks have been proposed internationally, in-
corporating indicators ranging from productivity and innovation to metrics on accessibility,
sustainability, and citizen participation (Shi; Shi, 2023). However, the simple importation of
foreign models can overlook local specicities, particularly in Global South contexts, where
structural challenges and historical inequalities require solutions adapted to territorial realities.
In this sense, it becomes essential to compare and understand both international and
national frameworks. Table 8.3 highlights the dierences and similarities between the widely
accepted six-domain framework (European Commission, 2020; Université de Liège, 2021) and
the ve-domain framework developed by (Fachinelli et al., 2023) for the Brazilian context. The
newer framework consolidates some domains to better reect local priorities and challenges,
connecting technology to people's daily lives. By aligning these indicators with regional realities
and priorities, a more solid path is created for smart cities to also become just cities.
The (Fachinelli et al., 2023) framework consolidates some of the traditional domains, such
as Smart Environment and Smart Mobility, into a single Sustainability and Accessibility ca-
tegory. This adaptation highlights the interconnectedness of these issues and emphasizes the
importance of a localized approach to smart city evaluation. It also reframes Smart People
to include Connectivity and Innovation, reecting the critical role of digital access and inno-
vation capacity in the Brazilian context. But the Smart People domain transcends mere
technical skills to encompass the civic capacities needed for democratic participation in digi-
tal urban governance (Bibri; Krogstie, 2020; Dameri; Benevolo, 2022). Educational initiatives
that foster data literacy and critical engagement become essential for transforming residents
from passive technology consumers to active co-creators of smart city solutions (Salas-Guerra,
2021). This educational transformation represents the keystone for equitable smart city de-
velopment. Without systemic investment in inclusive data-oriented education and capacity-
buildingeven through unplugged methodologies that teach core data concepts without re-
quiring a devicethe most sophisticated smart city infrastructures risk exacerbating existing
inequalities (D'Ignazio; Klein, 2023).
This is where the DSPE strategic approach to data-oriented education, which is both
intersectional and territorial, becomes paramount. By using "unplugged"tools and methodo-
logies, this form of education can build critical data literacy skills even in areas with limited
digital infrastructure. By rooting learning in local, real-world challenges, it empowers citizens

to understand and interpret urban data through a decolonial lens, enabling them to address
systemic inequities such as mobility and environmental injustice. When education systems
successfully cultivate these dual competenciesfostering both critical thinking and practical
skillsthey ensure that all smart city domains function as truly inclusive platforms for urban
transformation rather than as instruments of technological determinism.
8.4 DATA SCIENCE IN PUBLIC EDUCATION TERRITORIAL
METHODOLOGY
Originally launched in 2019 as the Girls in Data Science project by the Gamma Lab at
Federal University of Bahia (UFBA), this initiative evolved into the Data Science in Public
Education (DSPE) program in 2020 through a strategic partnership with the Itaú Foundation.
The program's strength lies in its territorial and pedagogical approach, which redenes how
data science is taught and applied. Instead of treating it as a purely technical discipline, DSPE
A Linguagem R na Sociedade 5.0 & Smart Cities Portal de Livros Abertos da USP,
Pirassununga: Faculdade de Zootecnia e Engenharia de Alimentos, 2026. 211 p. ISBN
978-65-87023-55-7 (e-book). Disponível em: https://doi.org/10.11606/9786587023557.

## Página 199

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 198
grounds data science within the social and environmental realities of each school community. It
is structured around three key phases that emphasize both plugged and unplugged educational
tools to ensure accessibility even in schools with limited technological infrastructure.
Phase 1: Understanding the Target Audience. This phase, known as the Enchantment Phase,
aims to sensitize students to the relevance of data science and stimulate their interest through
playful and participatory workshops. Simultaneously, a comprehensive diagnostic of the school
communities is conducted. This includes an initial assessment of both students' and teachers'
familiarity with STEM through surveys and focus groups to identify prior knowledge, interests,
and barriers to engagement. Historical performance data from INEP are also analyzed to con-
textualize students' baseline competencies. By combining emotional engagement with empirical
investigation, this phase ensures that subsequent pedagogical strategies are responsive to the
lived realities and needs of the school communities.
Phase 2: Formative and Informative Processes. The pedagogical model is grounded in project-
based learning (PBL), structured around nine thematic units that integrate data science with
real-world applications. The curriculum, adaptable for low-tech environments, emphasizes
hands-on activities, from data visualization to machine learning. Key components include
critical data literacy to analyze local issues, ethical frameworks to discuss algorithmic bias and
privacy, and interdisciplinary links with other subjects. The program hosts workshops on so-
cial, racial, and gender issues, fostering leadership and connecting participants to university
research labs and long-term pathways to higher education. A special focus is placed on gender
and racial equity, continuing the mission of the original Girls in Data Science project.
Phase 3: Connecting Theory and Practice. This phase transforms data science education into
an active, situated learning process through authentic research experiences. Students inves-
tigate problems rooted in their daily realities, positioning them as co-authors of knowledge.
This applied learning is centered on two main projects: City Data Exploration, where stu-
dents use the (re)Conhecendo Salvador platform to investigate local challenges; and Scientic
Inquiry, where they collaborate with university students on research projects grounded in their
own questions. These projects incorporate Freirean dialogic methods, ensuring that students'
observations and voices shape the research agenda.
The three phases of the DSPE methodology are summarized in Figure 8.3, which illus-
trates how the program moves from initial engagement to project-based learning and, nally,
to applied research.

Figura 8.3: Phases of the DSPE methodology: (1) Enchantment Phaseunderstanding the

ning, critical data literacy, and equity-centered pedagogy; (3) Connecting Theory and Prac-

Fonte: co-created by the author and Maíra Oliveira Esquerre (2025).
The mentioned (re)Conhecendo Salvador website (Gamma Lab., 2021) was developed as
a citizen engagement extension of the program, designed to support communities in collec-
ting, analyzing, and visualizing data about their own territories. Several toolssuch as QGIS,
IBGE GeoServices, OpenStreetMap, DataViva, SEEG, and the Atlas da Vulnerabilidade Cli-
máticawere used either directly or indirectly in the development of interactive maps, the
integration of geospatial data, and the creation of public-facing dashboards. Others, including
GoogleColab served as methodological references or sources of inspiration for designing parti-
cipatory features on the site. (re)Conhecendo Salvador enables students, teachers, and local
residents to contribute place-based knowledge and data, supporting community recognition and
territorial valorization while generating insights useful for public policy and urban planning.
By combining technical tools with community engagement, the platform exemplies how di-
gital infrastructures can be collaboratively designed to enhance data literacy and strengthen
territorial justice.
The program's comprehensive approach to teacher development and curriculum integra-
tion has been instrumental in its systemic impact. Through targeted workshops, educators gain
practical skills to adapt data science content for diverse classroom contexts, while also contri-
buting to the co-creation of instructional materials. These collaborative eorts have yielded
tangible institutional results, including the ocial adoption of DSPE-designed elective courses
in Bahia's state curriculum and the integration of foundational data science and AI concepts
into rst-year high school programs at pilot schools.
To sustain and democratize these educational advances, DSPE has developed a digital

## Página 201

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 200
infrastructure anchored by its public-facing Data Science in Education portal (Gamma
Lab., 2022). This dynamic platform serves as both repository and toolkit, integrating several
critical components: open-access curricular materials, annotated datasets for classroom use, do-
cumentation of successful student and teacher projects, and technical guides for implementing
open-source tools like R, Python, and QGIS in resource-constrained environments. The plat-
form's architecture intentionally blends direct utility with conceptual inspirationwhile some
tools are embedded for immediate use in data visualization and analysis, others are presented
as methodological models for adapting the program's approach to new contexts. This dual
function as both practical resource bank and proof-of-concept exemplies the program's com-
mitment to creating transferable, scalable solutions for data literacy education that maintain
delity to its core principles of accessibility and social relevance.
Finally, the integration of academic research with public education creates a powerful fe-
edback loop. Researchers and educators co-design activities that are both scientically rigorous
and pedagogically relevant. This collaboration ensures that the content is aligned with natio-
nal curricular guidelines, while also fostering innovation grounded in the lived experiences of
students. In this model, public schools become laboratories of environmental and social intel-
ligencespaces where students use data to map tree coverage in their neighborhoods, monitor
local pollution, or analyze public transportation ows. These projects not only generate valua-
ble insights for local governance but also demonstrate the transformative potential of education
when it is rooted in territory, equity, and participation.
Building on the locally adapted frameworks described earlier, it can be seen that the
DSPE territorial methodology brings these concepts to life in classrooms and communities. By
blending plugged and unplugged pedagogies, it translates smart city principles into accessible
and participatory educational practices. Other agship initiatives that apply this methodology
across diverse educational settings, scaling its impact and reach, are briey described in the
next section.
8.5 FLAGSHIP INITIATIVES DRIVING CHANGE
Building on the foundational success of the DSPE program, a series of interconnected
initiatives has emerged to deepen and diversify the impact of data literacy across educational
levels and social contexts. These initiatives are united by a common goal: to use data science
not only as a technical skillset but as a transformative tool for social justice, gender equity,
environmental sustainability, and civic engagement. Each program targets specic educational
gaps or societal challenges while remaining grounded in the DSPE philosophy of territorial
engagement, open knowledge, and collaborative learning. Together, these initiatives form a

## Página 202

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION
AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 201
vibrant ecosystem that extends the reach of data science education beyond traditional academic
boundariesinto neighborhoods, public infrastructure, and the daily lives of students as active
agents of change, beyond Bahia's state borders.
a. Girls in (Data) Science and AI: STEM for Social Justice
One of the most innovative branches of the DSPE program is its focus on gender equity
through the Girls in Data Science and Girls in (Data) Science and AI initiatives. Funded by
National Council for Scientic and Technological Development - CNPq's Calls No. 31/ 2018
and No. 31/2023, respectively, these project aims to challenge the structural underrepresen-
tation of women in STEM elds by fostering inclusive pathways for girls in public schools.
These initiatives provide mentorship opportunities, research grants, and hands-on projects co-
designed by students and educators. Girls in high school work closely with university students
to develop data oriented scientic research projects for real-world problems, such as school dro-
pout prediction, local transportation mapping, and environmental monitoring. This model not
only builds technical uency in data and AI but also frames STEM education as a vehicle for
social transformation. The upcoming 20252027 cycle is set to scale the initiative to new mu-
nicipal schools, further embedding a culture of gender justice, civic science, and technological
empowerment in public education localities.
b. Data Science in Engineering and Society: A Curriculum Innovation
On November 19, 2021, the course Data Science in Engineering and Society was approved
by the Curriculum Committee of the Polytechnic School at UFBA to become a required com-
ponent of the Chemical Engineering program, acknowledging the growing importance of data
science in professional training. With a semester load of 60 hours, the course benets from
the active participation of undergraduate and graduate researchers from the Gamma Lab. The
course has also been incorporated into the Graduate Program in Industrial Engineering as an
elective, with classes oered jointly to undergraduate and graduate students. This structure
fosters collaboration across levels of study and strengthens ties between students and rese-
archers engaged in the Data Science in Public Education Program and its diverse outreach
initiatives.Through this curriculum component, students deepen their knowledge and skills in
data science theory, statistical learning, and machine learning tools, while also experiencing
how academic research can connect directly to public education and societal impact.
In alignment with these eorts, cooperation agreements have been established and forma-
lized between industries and Gamma Lab. with the aim of promoting technical-scientic and
cultural collaboration through the sharing of data and information for teaching, research, and
extension activities. These agreements foster the development and dissemination of knowledge

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL EDUCATION

while also promoting youth training, academic publishing, and institutional planning and de-
velopment. Joint actions span teaching, research, and extension at both undergraduate and
graduate levels, reinforcing academic and scientic activities and creating new opportunities
for learning and innovation.
c. Engineering, Science, Ethics, and Social Justice: Integrating teaching, research,
and extension
A growing dimension of the DSPE ecosystem is the integration of data science, ethics,
and social justice into engineering education. After three semesters of experimentation, in 2024
the Department of Chemical Engineering formally approved the extension courses Engenharia,
Ciência, Ética e Justiça Social I e II (Engineering, Science, Ethics, and Social Justice I and II),
oered as special topics across two consecutive semesters. Over 210 hours of theorypractice ac-
tivities, undergraduate and graduate students design collaborative scientic and entrepreneurial
initiatives in their communities, developing proposals in the rst semester and implementing
them in the second. These projects often take the form of science clubs in nearby public schools
or partnerships with local small businesses, creating spaces for youth-led research, mentoring,
and innovation. This reciprocal model deepens students' understanding of the social dimen-
sions of engineering while democratizing access to tools and knowledge usually restricted to
higher education. By integrating teaching, research, and extension, the initiative fosters social
innovation, local development, and alignment with the UN Sustainable Development Goals,
particularly quality education, reduced inequalities, and sustainable cities.
d. Vocational High School Program in Data Science
In partnership with SENAC-SP, the DSPE initiative has launched Brazil's rst vocational
high school track dedicated entirely to data science in 2024. This program represents a paradigm
shift in technical education, embedding skills in Python programming, statistical modeling, and
geospatial analysis directly into the high school curriculum. Beyond technical literacy, students
are encouraged to apply their learning to address challenges in their local communitieswhether
by mapping ood-prone areas, analyzing school transportation routes, or developing dashboards
for neighborhood health data. By oering a robust and practical curriculum that is both
relevant and socially engaged, the program prepares students for future employment in data-
intensive elds while also strengthening their role as informed citizens capable of contributing to
sustainable urban development. The program exemplies how vocational education can evolve
to meet the demands of a data-driven society without abandoning its public mission.
e. Postgraduate Research: Environmental Data Science
DSPE's impact also extends to the realm of postgraduate research, where data science is

harnessed to address pressing environmental challenges in urban contexts. For instance, PhD
and MSc. research focus on modeling emerging contaminants, such as pharmaceutical residues
in urban water systems. By using machine learning techniques alongside SHAP (SHapley
Additive exPlanations) analysis, researchers are able to identify key variables inuencing the
presence and distribution of pollutants, generating insights with direct implications for public
health and regulatory frameworks. Another PhD research applies recurrent neural networks
(RNNs) to detect leaks in water distribution systems. These AI models allow for the early
identication of anomalies in water ow, enabling preventive maintenance and reducing losses
in urban infrastructure. Both research eorts exemplify how advanced data science techniques
can contribute to low-cost, scalable solutions for cities, while also promoting environmental
justice and sustainability. They also reinforce the DSPE program's multilevel strategy: building
capacity from secondary education through to advanced research, with a consistent focus on
societal relevance and territorial impact. The transformative reach of DSPE is perhaps best
illustrated not only through institutional frameworks but also through individual experiences,
as shown in Figure 8.4. The image emphasizes how students embody data science learning in
everyday spaces, blurring the boundaries between academic settings and their lived realities.
By applying the DSPE methodology across diverse contexts, the agship initiatives de-
monstrate how theory is translated into tangible action. Each program adapts its core principles
to address specic societal challenges, ranging from gender equity in STEM to vocational trai-
ning in data science. Serving as bridges between educational frameworks and lived experience,
these initiativeshighlighted in Section 6bring personal stories to the forefront through the
voices of students, teachers, and researchers who have experienced this transformation.
Drawing on the experiences developed in Salvador, Table 8.4 connects the domains of the
Brazilian Smart Cities framework with the actions and initiatives of the DSPE program and
their related projects. It illustrates how these activities can advance social inclusion, sustaina-
bility, and innovation by outlining each domain's primary focus, its specic contributions, and
the potential positive impacts on building more equitable and resilient smart cities.
8.6 STORIES THAT INSPIRE
The impact of the DSPE program is perhaps best understood through the reections of
those who experienced it. Participants from dierent levelshigh school students, undergradu-
ate mentors, and graduate researchersconsistently describe how engaging with data reshaped
their worldview. Data was no longer abstract; it became a tool for agency and intervention,
helping them connect knowledge to challenges of their communities.
For a 19-year-old student who began her journey as a high school researcher and later
became a Gamma Research Fellow, the shift was profound: My entire perspective now comes

8.7 CONCLUSION: TOWARD JUST AND INTELLIGENT CITIES
This chapter's central argument is that a truly "smart"city is not dened by the sophisti-
cation of its technology, but by the intelligence, engagement, and empowerment of its citizenry.
The experiences and initiatives grounded on Data Science in Public Education (DSPE) program
in Salvador, Brazil, stand as inspiring examples of how this vision can be realized. By rejecting
a top-down, technocentric model and instead embedding data literacy in the territorial realities
and social needs of marginalized communities, DSPE demonstrates that education is the most
critical infrastructure for an equitable urban future.
Through initiatives that connect students, teachers, and university researchers, DSPE
transforms data from an abstract resource into a practical tool for civic engagement, social
advocacy, and democratic participation. These eorts prove that when people are empowered
to understand, interpret, and act on data, they gain the capacity to challenge systemic ine-
quities and co-create cities that are more inclusive, just, and resilient. The success stories of
young researchers and former students are not isolated achievementsthey are indicators of
a replicable and scalable model. They show that investing in inclusive, territorially grounded
data literacy is not simply an educational strategy, but a pathway to urban transformation.
Moreover, the broader set of agship initiatives described in Section 5including Girls
in (Data) Science, Science Clubs and Engineering for Justice, the Vocational High School
Program in Data Science, and postgraduate environmental data researchdemonstrates that

## Página 208

CAPÍTULO 8. (DATA) SCIENCE FOR SOCIAL JUSTICE: TERRITORIAL
EDUCATION AND TOOLS FOR SMART AND EQUITABLE CITIES 207
these results are not conned to DSPE alone. Together, these programs form a robust ecosystem
that addresses multiple dimensions of social justice, gender equity, environmental sustainability,
and economic opportunity. They reveal that aligning education, research, and community
engagement can produce systemic change that resonates far beyond a single program's scope.
The future of truly intelligent cities lies not in sensors and algorithms, but in the collective
wisdom, creativity, and purposeful action of informed and engaged citizens. The experiences,
strategies, and multi-level initiatives presented in this chapter underscore a powerful lesson:
technological innovation must be aligned with educational equity and community participation.
This alignment is not optionalit is essential to ensure that the smart cities of tomorrow
are also the just cities our societies need today. Ultimately, it is through this process that
citizens are empowered to embrace the transformative journey of to learn and to experiment,
in order to understand and to transform (from the Portuguese: Aprender e experimentar para
compreender e transformar, Karla Esquerre & Roseline Oliveira).
Acknowledgments
This work would not have been possible without the tireless eorts of the researchers
and faculty of Gamma Lab at UFBA. We are also eternally grateful to our partner instituti-
ons, whose collaboration has been essential in building the Data Science in Public Education
(DSPE) program and its related initiatives. Our sincere thanks also go to the teachers, school
sta, and families whose commitment brought this program to life. This research was sup-
ported by funding and scholarships from the National Council for Scientic and Technological
Development (CNPq), the Ministry of Science, Technology, and Innovation (MCTIC), the Itaú
Foundation for Education and Culture, the Coordination for the Improvement of Higher Edu-
cation Personnel (CAPES), and the Bahia State Research Support Foundation (FAPESB). We
also acknowledge the support of Stanford University's Visiting Scholars Program (2023) and
CNPq's Research Productivity Fellow Program (2025).
On a personal note, I am profoundly grateful to Cedma Ranielly Santos Firmino, PhD
candidate, for her invaluable insights, suggestions, and meticulous revisions of this book chapter;
and to Maíra Oliveira Esquerre, for her particular work in translating the chapter's complex
ideas into clear and engaging visual components (Figures 1-5), which were co-produced with
the author.
